28

29

30
when you're introducing somebody.
31

32

33

34

35

37

39

42

16

--- SLIDES THAT WORK: M. for Minimalism ---

1
Let's talk about minimalism.
2
Less is more.
3
Simplicity works best.
4
Making slides that have minimal detail on them
5
is not for every occasion
6
as complex software program
7
or engineering related presentations
8
can be tough to minimalize, okay?
9
But you can do it and it's actually less work.
10
It's easier to understand as well, okay.
11
And just as a side note.
12

13
in the case study section of this act
14
when we talk about using animation to build upon
15
and explain very complex topics.
16

17
I wanna stress that I really believe that less is more
18
in business communications
19
and I want you to really try hard
20
to never have more than three bullet points on a slide
21
because we just can't retain more than three things
22
at a time, right?
23
It is what it is
24
and so try to experiment with even less than three ideas
25
on a slide if you can.
26
Simplicity works extraordinarily well, right?
27
And we're conditioned
28
to not read instruction manuals anymore
29
or to hit that F1 key on a keyboard for help, right?
30
My kids don't even know what that means F1, right?
31
The way it works now is if it's not intuitive
32
then you gotta rethink it please.
33
Just keep it simple.
34
Kinda like this device that Steve Jobs pioneered.
35
It's just got one button, right?
36
There's no instruction manual on how to use this.
37
And I used to be a software programmer for years
38
and we used to create these complex
39
client server-based applications, right?
40
But what's happened is in software,
41
all you gotta know how to use is a browser.
42
Forward, back, home, stop, refresh.
43
That's it, right?
44
You never really have to learn how to use a website, right?
45
If it's not intuitive then people won't pay attention
46
so if it's not intuitive,
47
please rethink your slide structure.
48
Just keep it simple, right?
49
And use simple language as well, okay?
50
Steve Jobs was amazing at this, right?
51
He used to use words like insanely great on his slides.
52
And one online magazine company that I wrote for
53
actually asked me to write at an eighth grade level.
54
I know it sounds out there,
55
but I guess minimalism works, right?

--- SLIDES THAT WORK: C. for Consistency ---

1
C is for consistency.
2
If you use a certain theme in your slides,
3
make sure to stay true to that consistent theme.
4
Kinda like how your belt is suppose
5
to match your shoes.
6
And we'll talk about dressing
7
and what to wear at presentations
8
later on in Act number 9.
9
But please try to be consistent
10
if it makes sense to do so,
11
you know with your fonts, with the colors you use,
12
with story telling themes, etc.
13
And it can be a lot of fun too.
14
So, when I gave my TEDx talk,
15
I used a them of TEA, T-E-A, TEA.
16
TEA solving all problems in the world,
17
Technology, Education and Acceptance.
18
And I included many quotes about tea,
19
which was a lot of fun.
20
And it was unique, it was different as well,
21
I had a good time with it.
22
So, stay true to your theme if you want to.
23
And some presenters stay true
24
to a particular theme in virtually
25
every presentation they do.
26
Like Steve Jobs did per these slides here.
27
I personally love having different themes
28
for all of my presentations,
29
as I love to kinda think of each one
30
as a different experience.
31
And, great musicians and film directors
32
really mix up their presentations
33
or their masterpieces,
34
and they have different themes
35
for different things that they create.
36
You know, retro themes can work well as well.
37
Or a period piece where a theme stays
38
in the same historical time period
39
in order to remain consistent.
40
Here's some examples that have
41
a thematic historical consistency to them.
42
Audiences love consistency
43
and themes that they appreciate.
44
And they really appreciate the hard work
45
that you put into it to stay consistent with the theme.
46
They see it as art and everybody loves art.
47
And this will really help for you to stand out as well.
48
Now, when it comes to the tense that you use on your slides,
49
please be consistent as well.
50
Kinda like on your resume.
51
For all of your historical work experience,
52
you write them in the past tense,
53
like managed a group of people
54
or created an Oscar worthy presentation, which you will.
55
Also, try to stick with one or two fonts as well,
56
if you want, as it can kinda be distracting
57
if you use too many different types of fonts
58
when you're quoting people from different periods
59
that sort of thing, etc.
60
It's up to you, it's up to you.
61
Play around with it and just have fun.

2

16

24

--- Pausing ---

1
Mark Twain once said that the right word
2
may be effective,
3
but no word was ever as effective
4
as a rightly timed pause.
5
Okay, that pause was too long.
6
But pausing after you say something controversial
7
or important
8
or the most important thing you say in a speech is crucial
9
as it allows people to digest what you just said.
10
Okay, that was the right amount of time for a pause.
11
Pause also between topics.
12
It kinda creates contrast and lets people know
13
that you're moving on to something else.
14
I guess the best way to think about a pause in a speech
15
is when you're writing an essay
16
and you put a period and then a space,
17
which is just a small pause in between two sentences.
18
And then, when you talk about a brand new topic,
19
it's a much bigger space,
20
which means you have a new paragraph,
21
so it's kinda like that.
22
Think of it that way.
23
And so, if you give a very long presentation,
24
quite often, I like to teach three to four hour
25
in class university sessions,
26
I usually give a break every one and a half to two hours,
27
but pausing when you're talking in general,
28
in between important points,
29
it ensures your audience
30
not only retains the content of your presentation,
31
but it gives them time to process it.
32
Thanks.
